import re
from typing import Union

_UNITS = {...}
_TOKEN_RE = re.compile(r'(\d+(?:\.\d+)?)\s*([a-z]+)?\s*')

def parse_duration(text: str) -> Union[int, float]:
    """Parse duration strings like '1h30m' into seconds.
    Supported units: d, h, m, s, ms (plus common full forms). Case-insensitive.
    """
    if not isinstance(text, str):
        raise TypeError(f"expected str, got {type(text).__name__}")
    s = text.strip().lower()
    if not s:
        raise ValueError("duration string is empty")

    sign = 1
    if s[0] in "+-":
        sign = -1 if s[0] == "-" else 1
        s = s[1:].strip()
        if not s:
            raise ValueError("duration string contains only a sign")

    total = 0.0
    pos = 0
    n = len(s)
    tokens = 0

    while pos < n:
        while pos < n and s[pos].isspace():
            pos += 1
        if pos == n:
            break
        m = _TOKEN_RE.match(s, pos)
        if not m:
            raise ValueError(f"invalid duration string: {text!r}")
        number_str, unit = m.groups()
        pos = m.end()
        tokens += 1
        try:
            value = float(number_str)
        except ValueError:
            raise ValueError(...)
        if value < 0: # not possible
            ...
        if unit is None or unit == "":
            factor = 1.0
        else:
            factor = _UNITS.get(unit)
            if factor is None:
                raise ValueError(f"unknown unit {unit!r} in duration string {text!r}")
        total += value * factor

    if tokens == 0:
        raise ValueError(...)
    total *= sign
    return int(total) if total.is_integer() else total

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
dedup.py -- remove duplicate lines while preserving order.
Usage: python dedup.py [-o OUT] [--ignore-case] [FILE]
If FILE is omitted, reads from stdin.
"""
import argparse
import sys

def main() -> None:
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Remove duplicate lines from a file or stdin, preserving first occurrence order.")
    parser.add_argument("file", nargs="?", default=None, help="Input file (reads stdin if omitted)")
    parser.add_argument("-o", "--output", help="Output file (default: stdout)")
    parser.add_argument("--ignore-case", action="store_true", help="Treat lines as duplicates ignoring case")
    args = parser.parse_args()

    if args.file is None:
        infile = sys.stdin
    else:
        infile = open(args.file, "r", encoding="utf-8", newline="")

    if args.output:
        outfile = open(args.output, "w", encoding="utf-8", newline="")
    else:
        outfile = sys.stdout

    seen = set()
    try:
        for line in infile:
            key = line.rstrip("\r\n")
            if args.ignore_case:
                key = key.lower()
            if key in seen:
                continue
            seen.add(key)
            outfile.write(line)
    finally:
        if infile is not sys.stdin:
            infile.close()
        if outfile is not sys.stdout:
            outfile.close()

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

import re
_UNIT_SECONDS = {'d':86400, 'h':3600, 'm':60, 's':1, 'ms':0.001}
_PATTERN = re.compile(r'\s*((?:\d+(?:\.\d+)?\s*(?:d|ms|h|m|s)\s*)+)\s*')
def parse_duration(value: str) -> float:
    if not isinstance(value, str): raise TypeError...
    m = _PATTERN.fullmatch(value)
    if not m: raise ValueError...
    result=0.0
    for number, unit in re.findall(r'(\d+(?:\.\d+)?)\s*(ms|d|h|m|s)', m.group(1)):
        result += float(number)*_UNIT_SECONDS[unit]
    # If result integer and original no decimal? Could return int if result.is_integer()? Maybe if all numbers integers and unit not ms? For ms could be fractional. Simpler: return result if not integer? But type consistency? Could return int when no fractional part. But if '1.0s', result integer. Maybe acceptable.
    return int(result) if result.is_integer() else result

import re
from typing import Union

_DURATION_RE = re.compile(
    r'\s*((?:\d+(?:\.\d+)?\s*[A-Za-z]+\s*)+)\s*'
)
_UNIT_SECONDS = {
    'd': 86400, 'day': 86400, 'days': 86400,
    'h': 3600, 'hr': 3600, 'hrs': 3600, 'hour': 3600, 'hours': 3600,
    'm': 60, 'min': 60, 'mins': 60, 'minute': 60, 'minutes': 60,
    's': 1, 'sec': 1, 'secs': 1, 'second': 1, 'seconds': 1,
    'ms': 0.001, 'millisecond': 0.001, 'milliseconds': 0.001,
}

def parse_duration(value: str) -> Union[int, float]:
    """Parse a duration string such as '1h30m', '45s', or '1d 2h 30ms' into seconds.

    Raises ValueError if the string is empty, contains non-numeric parts,
    has unknown units, or is negative. Returns int when the result is whole,
    otherwise float.
    """
    if not isinstance(value, str):
         raise TypeError(...)
    text = value.strip()
    if not text:
         raise ValueError("duration string cannot be empty")
    if '-' in text or '+' in text? If text includes negative sign pattern fullmatch fails because `[A-Za-z]+` and `\d` no sign. Could simply rely fullmatch. But if text '-1h' fullmatch fails. Good.
    m = _DURATION_RE.fullmatch(text)
    if not m: raise ValueError(f"Invalid duration: {value!r}")
    total = 0.0
    parts = re.findall(r'(\d+(?:\.\d+)?)\s*([A-Za-z]+)', m.group(1))
    if not parts: raise ValueError ... (not needed)
    for num, unit in parts:
       unit = unit.lower()
       if unit not in _UNIT_SECONDS:
            raise ValueError(f"Unknown unit {unit!r} in duration {value!r}")
       total += float(num) * _UNIT_SECONDS[unit]
    return int(total) if total.is_integer() else total

```python
import re
from typing import Union

_BARE_NUMBER_RE = re.compile(r"\d+(?:\.\d+)?")
_DURATION_RE = re.compile(r"\s*((?:\d+(?:\.\d+)?\s*[A-Za-z]+\s*)+)\s*")
_PART_RE = re.compile(r"(\d+(?:\.\d+)?)\s*([A-Za-z]+)")

_UNIT_SECONDS = {
    "d": 86400,
    "day": 86400,
    "days": 86400,
    "h": 3600,
    "hr": 3600,
    "hrs": 3600,
    "hour": 3600,
    "hours": 3600,
    "m": 60,
    "min": 60,
    "mins": 60,
    "minute": 60,
    "minutes": 60,
    "s": 1,
    "sec": 1,
    "secs": 1,
    "second": 1,
    "seconds": 1,
    "ms": 0.001,
    "millis": 0.001,
    "millisecond": 0.001,
    "milliseconds": 0.001,
}

def parse_duration(value: str) -> Union[int, float]:
    """..."""
    if not isinstance(value, str):
        raise TypeError("value must be a string")

    text = value.strip()
    if not text:
        raise ValueError("duration string must not be empty")

    if _BARE_NUMBER_RE.fullmatch(text):
        number = float(text)
        return int(number) if number.is_integer() else number

    match = _DURATION_RE.fullmatch(text)
    if not match:
        raise ValueError(f"invalid duration: {value!r}")

    total = 0.0
    for number, unit in _PART_RE.findall(match.group(1)):
        unit = unit.lower()
        if unit not in _UNIT_SECONDS:
            raise ValueError(f"unknown unit {unit!r} in duration: {value!r}")
        total += float(number) * _UNIT_SECONDS[unit]

    return int(total) if total.is_integer() else total
```

Potential issue: If text is '123.000' float -> 123.0 is_integer True -> int. good.

Potential script:
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Remove duplicate lines from a file, preserving first occurrence order."""
import argparse
import sys

def strip_line_ending(line: str) -> str:
    # preserve original line for output, normalize key
    if line.endswith('\n'):
        body = line[:-1]
    else:
        body = line
    if body.endswith('\r'):
        body = body[:-1]
    return body

def main():
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Remove duplicate lines while preserving order.")
    parser.add_argument("file", help="Input file ('-' for stdin)")
    parser.add_argument("-o", "--output", help="Output file (default: stdout)")
    parser.add_argument("--ignore-case", action="store_true", help="Ignore case when comparing lines")
    args = parser.parse_args()

    def open_input():
        if args.file == '-':
            return sys.stdin
        return open(args.file, 'r', encoding='utf-8', errors='replace')  # maybe surrogate? better errors? use text=True default? Could use encoding='utf-8' and errors='replace'.

    def open_output():
        if args.output:
            return open(args.output, 'w', encoding='utf-8', newline='') # preserve original line endings? if writing original line, newline='' prevents translation
        return sys.stdout

    seen = set()
    with open_input() as fin, open_output() as fout:
        for line in fin:
            key = strip_line_ending(line)
            if args.ignore_case:
                key = key.casefold()
            if key not in seen:
                seen.add(key)
                fout.write(line)

if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()

But if output stdout, with sys.stdout fine? It closes stdout? Usually fine. But if args.output none, open_output returns sys.stdout. Could avoid context for stdin? It's okay. But if input file is '-' and output is stdout, no problem. Could simpler: if args.file == '-': fin = sys.stdin else open...; if args.output: fout = open(...) else sys.stdout. no with? #!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
dedup_lines.py: remove duplicate lines from a file, preserving the first occurrence order.

Usage:
    python dedup_lines.py input.txt > output.txt
    python dedup_lines.py input.txt --ignore-case -o output.txt
    cat input.txt | python dedup_lines.py - --ignore-case
"""

import argparse
import sys


def line_key(line: str) -> str:
    # Ignore trailing line-ending characters for comparison, but preserve original line when writing.
    if line.endswith("\n"):
        line = line[:-1]
    if line.endswith("\r"):
        line = line[:-1]
    return line


def main() -> None:
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Remove duplicate lines while preserving original order.")
    parser.add_argument("file", help="input file, or '-' for standard input")
    parser.add_argument("-o", "--output", help="write to this file instead of standard output")
    parser.add_argument("--ignore-case", action="store_true", help="ignore case when comparing lines")
    args = parser.parse_args()

    if args.file == "-":
        fin = sys.stdin
    else:
        fin = open(args.file, "r", encoding="utf-8", newline="")

    if args.output:
        fout = open(args.output, "w", encoding="utf-8", newline="")
    else:
        fout = sys.stdout

    seen = set()
    try:
        for line in fin:
            key = line_key(line)
            if args.ignore_case:
                key = key.casefold()

            if key not in seen:
                seen.add(key)
                fout.write(line)
    finally:
        if args.file != "-":
            fin.close()
        if args.output:
            fout.close()
        # Do not close sys.stdin/sys.stdout explicitly here? Actually if fout is sys.stdout, closing in finally could close stdout. We only close if args.output. For fin if not stdin. Good.

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

    text = str(text).strip().lower()
    if not text: raise ValueError

    tokens = re.findall(r'(\d+(?:\.\d+)?)(ms|d|h|m|s)', text)

Potential final:
from __future__ import annotations
import re

def parse_duration(value: str) -> int | float:
    """
    Parse a duration string into seconds.

    Supports optional day/hour/minute/second components, e.g. '1h30m', '90s',
    '2d4h', '1.5m'. Case-insensitive. Bare numbers are treated as seconds.
    """
    if not isinstance(value, str):
        raise TypeError("duration must be a string")

    text = value.strip().lower()
    text = re.sub(r"\s+", "", text)

    if not text:
        raise ValueError("duration cannot be empty")

    unit_seconds = {
        "d": 86_400,
        "h": 3_600,
        "m": 60,
        "s": 1,
    }

    # Optional bare numeric value: treat as seconds.
    if re.fullmatch(r"\d+(?:\.\d+)?", text):
        return int(float(text)) if float(text).is_integer() else float(text)

    pattern = re.compile(r"(\d+(?:\.\d+)?)(d|h|m|s)")
    total = 0.0
    pos = 0
    matched = 0

    for m in pattern.finditer(text):
        if m.start() != pos:
            raise ValueError(f"Invalid duration: {value!r}")
        number = float(m.group(1))
        unit = m.group(2)
        total += number * unit_seconds[unit]
        pos = m.end()
        matched += 1

    if matched == 0 or pos != len(text):
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid duration: {value!r}")

    return int(total) if total.is_integer() else total

```
import re

_DURATION_RE = re.compile(r"(\d+(?:\.\d+)?)(ms|d|h|m|s)")
_UNIT_SECONDS = {"d": 86_400, "h": 3_600, "m": 60, "s": 1, "ms": 0.001}

def parse_duration(value: str) -> int | float:
    """
    Parse a duration string into seconds.

    Examples:
        parse_duration('1h30m')  # 5400
        parse_duration('45s')    # 45
        parse_duration('2d4h')   # 187200

    Accepts case-insensitive units: d, h, m, s, ms. Spaces are ignored.
    A bare number is treated as seconds. Raises ValueError for invalid input.
    """
    if not isinstance(value, str):
        raise TypeError("duration must be a string")

    text = re.sub(r"\s+", "", value.strip().lower())
    if not text:
        raise ValueError("duration cannot be empty")

    if re.fullmatch(r"\d+(?:\.\d+)?", text):
        seconds = float(text)
        return int(seconds) if seconds.is_integer() else seconds

    total = 0
    pos = 0

    for match in _DURATION_RE.finditer(text):
        if match.start() != pos:
            raise ValueError(f"Invalid duration: {value!r}")

        number_text = match.group(1)
        unit = match.group(2)
        number = int(number_text) if "." not in number_text else float(number_text)
        total += number * _UNIT_SECONDS[unit]
        pos = match.end()

    if pos != len(text):
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid duration: {value!r}")

    return int(total) if total.is_integer() else total
```

Let's consider robust script:

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Remove duplicate lines from a file while preserving order."""

import argparse
import sys


def main(argv=None):
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(
        description="Remove duplicate lines from a file while preserving the original order."
    )
    parser.add_argument(
        "input",
        nargs="?",
        help="input file (defaults to stdin)",
    )
    parser.add_argument(
        "-o", "--output",
        help="write to this file instead of stdout",
    )
    parser.add_argument(
        "--ignore-case",
        action="store_true",
        help="treat lines that differ only by letter case as duplicates",
    )
    args = parser.parse_args(argv)

    if args.input:
        try:
            infile = open(args.input, "r", encoding="utf-8", errors="replace", newline="")
        except OSError as e:
            parser.error(f"cannot open {args.input}: {e}")
    else:
        infile = sys.stdin

    if args.output:
        try:
            outfile = open(args.output, "w", encoding="utf-8", newline="")
        except OSError as e:
            parser.error(f"cannot open {args.output}: {e}")
    else:
        outfile = sys.stdout

    seen = set()
    try:
        for line in infile:
            key = line.rstrip("\r\n")
            if args.ignore_case:
                key = key.casefold()
            if key not in seen:
                seen.add(key)
                outfile.write(line)
    finally:
        if infile is not sys.stdin:
            infile.close()
        if outfile is not sys.stdout:
            outfile.close()

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Check parser.error exits with status 2. If input file omitted and output file specified, reads stdin. Good.

Let's write polished script:

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Remove duplicate lines from a file while preserving original order."""

import argparse
import sys


def line_key(line: str, ignore_case: bool) -> str:
    # Remove only the line ending used for comparison.
    if line.endswith("\n"):
        line = line[:-1]
    if line.endswith("\r"):
        line = line[:-1]
    return line.casefold() if ignore_case else line


def main(argv=None) -> int:
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(
        description="Remove duplicate lines while preserving the first occurrence order."
    )
    parser.add_argument("input", nargs="?", help="input file; read stdin if omitted")
    parser.add_argument("-o", "--output", help="output file; default stdout")
    parser.add_argument("--ignore-case", action="store_true", help="ignore case when comparing lines")
    args = parser.parse_args(argv)

    if args.input:
        try:
            infile = open(args.input, "r", encoding="utf-8", errors="replace")
        except OSError as e:
            print(f"error: cannot open {args.input}: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
            return 1
    else:
        infile = sys.stdin

    if args.output:
        try:
            outfile = open(args.output, "w", encoding="utf-8")
        except OSError as e:
            print(f"error: cannot open {args.output}: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
            if infile is not sys.stdin:
                infile.close()
            return 1
    else:
        outfile = sys.stdout

    seen = set()
    try:
        for line in infile:
            key = line_key(line, args.ignore_case)
            if key not in seen:
                seen.add(key)
                outfile.write(line)
    except OSError as e:
        print(f"error: I/O error: {e}", file=sys.stderr)
        return 1
    finally:
        if infile is not sys.stdin:
            infile.close()
        if outfile is not sys.stdout:
            outfile.close()

    return 0


if __name__ == "__main__":
    sys.exit(main())

Maybe add --encoding? More code. Keep simple.

Could use line_key with line.strip()? No, don't strip spaces. We remove only line ending. Good.

Need maybe if --ignore-case, use casefold for Unicode. Good.

- At least one token required

- `""` → ValueError
- `"1h30m"` → 5400
- `"45s"` → 45

- `"1.5h"` → 5400
- `"500ms"` → 0.5
- `"1h 30m"` → 5400 (allow spaces)
- `"abc"` → ValueError

- Non-string input → TypeError

Let me write this out:

```python
import re

def parse_duration(s: str) -> int | float:
    """Parse a duration string like '1h30m', '45s', '2d3h' into seconds.
    
    Supported units: d (days), h (hours), m (minutes), s (seconds), ms (milliseconds).
    Returns int for whole-second values, float when milliseconds are present.
    """
    if not isinstance(s, str):
        raise TypeError(f"Expected str, got {type(s).__name__}")
    
    s = s.strip()
    if not s:
        raise ValueError("Duration string is empty")
    
    # Find all (number, unit) pairs; allow optional whitespace between them
    pattern = re.compile(r'(\d+(?:\.\d+)?)(ms|d|h|m|s)')
    matches = pattern.findall(s)
    
    if not matches:
        raise ValueError(f"Could not parse duration: {s!r}")
    
    # Verify the string is fully consumed by the matches (allowing whitespace)
    reconstructed = ''.join(num + unit for num, unit in matches)
    # Compare ignoring whitespace
    if s.replace(' ', '') != reconstructed:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid duration format: {s!r}")
    
    multipliers = {
        'd': 86400,
        'h': 3600,
        'm': 60,
        's': 1,
        'ms': 0.001,
    }
    
    total = 0
    has_ms = False
    for num_str, unit in matches:
        value = float(num_str)
        total += value * multipliers[unit]
        if unit == 'ms':
            has_ms = True
    
    return round(total, 6) if has_ms else int(total)
```

Wait, the reconstruction check: if the input is `"1h 30m"`, then `s.replace(' ', '')` = `"1h30m"`, and reconstructed = `"1h30m"`. Good.

But what about `"1h30ms"`? The regex would match `(\d+(?:\.\d+)?)(ms|d|h|m|s)`. For "30ms", it would match "30" and "ms" because "ms" is listed before "m" in the alternation. Good.

But wait: what about `"1h30ms"`? The regex with `findall` would find:
- "1", "h" 
- "30", "ms"
Yes! Because the regex engine at position after "1h" sees "30ms" and matches "30" then tries "ms" first (since it's first in the alternation), matches. 

What about `"1h30m"` (no s)? 
- "1", "h"
- "30", "m"
Yes, "m" matches.

What about tricky case: `"1ms"` → "1", "ms". Good.
`"1m"` → "1", "m". Good.

Now the reconstruction check: for `"1h30ms"`, reconstructed = "1h30ms", s.replace(' ','') = "1h30ms". ✓

What about `"1h 30 m"`? s.replace(' ','') = "1h30m", but the regex would find "1","h" and "30","m" (the space before m is fine since findall just finds matches anywhere). reconstructed = "1h30m". ✓

What about `"1 h 30 m"`? Same thing. ✓

What about `"1h30m "` (trailing space)? We strip first, so it becomes "1h30m". ✓

What about `"1h x 30m"`? s.replace(' ','') = "1hx30m", reconstructed = "1h30m". Not equal → ValueError. ✓

What about just a number `"123"`? No match → ValueError. Good, requires unit.

I think this is solid. Let me also add a docstring with examples and maybe a small test block.

Actually, let me reconsider: should I use `re.fullmatch` approach instead? Like matching the whole string at once?

Alternative: `re.fullmatch(r'(\d+(?:\.\d+)?)(?:ms|d|h|m|s)(\d+(?:\.\d+)?)(?:ms|d|h|m|s)*...', s)` — this gets unwieldy. The findall + reconstruction check is cleaner.

One more edge: what about very long digit strings? Python handles big ints natively, and we convert to float. For extremely large durations this could lose precision, but that's an acceptable limitation.

What about `"0h"`? → 0. Fine, valid.
What about `"00s"`? → 0. Fine.
What about `".5h"`? The regex requires `\d+` before optional decimal, so ".5" won't match. That's a reasonable restriction. If the user wants "0.5h" they should write it that way.

Question: Write a small Python command-line tool that removes duplicate lines from a file while preserving the original order, with an --ignore-case flag. Give me the script.

Thinking:

- Read all lines

- Standard argparse for CLI
- Read file, process, write to stdout or output file

- Add -o for output

Let me think about the design:
- Input: file path (positional argument)
- --ignore-case: flag to make comparison case-insensitive

- Preserve order: keep first occurrence, skip subsequent duplicates

Let me draft the script:

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""dedup: Remove duplicate lines from a file, preserving order."""

import argparse
import sys


def dedup(lines, ignore_case=False):
    seen = set()
    result = []
    for line in lines:
        key = line.lower() if ignore_case else line
        if key not in seen:
            seen.add(key)
            result.append(line)
    return result


def main():
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Remove duplicate lines while preserving order.")
    parser.add_argument("file", nargs="?", help="Input file (reads stdin if omitted)")
    parser.add_argument("-i", "--ignore-case", action="store_true", help="Case-insensitive comparison")
    parser.add_argument("-o", "--output", help="Write to file instead of stdout")
    args = parser.parse_args()

    if args.file:
        with open(args.file, "r") as f:
            lines = f.readlines()
    else:
        lines = sys.stdin.readlines()

    result = dedup(lines, args.ignore_case)

    if args.output:
        with open(args.output, "w") as f:
            f.writelines(result)
    else:
        sys.stdout.writelines(result)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

This is clean, small, functional. Let me think about edge cases:
- Trailing newlines: readlines() preserves them, so that's fine
- Empty file: works fine
- No file given: reads stdin - nice bonus
- The key for ignore_case should be the line with its newline? Actually, let me think... if line is "Hello\n" and "hello\n", with ignore_case they should be considered duplicates. Using line.lower() gives "hello\n" for both, so that works.

Actually, should I strip the newline for comparison? No - if one line is "Hello" (no newline) and another is "Hello\n" (with newline), they're technically different lines. But in practice, files have consistent newlines. I'll keep it simple and compare the full line including newline. Actually, let me reconsider - it's more user-friendly to strip for comparison but preserve the original for output. Let me do:

Hmm, but that changes semantics. If a file has "Hello\n" and "Hello" (last line without newline), with ignore_case they'd be duplicates. Without ignore_case they wouldn't. That seems like a reasonable behavior.

Actually, let me keep it simple. The user asked for a small tool. I'll compare the full line (including newline) for exact match, and the lowercased full line for ignore-case. This is the most predictable behavior.

Wait, let me reconsider. If I have:
```
Hello

hello
```
With --ignore-case, all three are duplicates of each other. Keep first "Hello". That's correct.

If I have:
```

```
Without --ignore-case, both are kept. Correct.

I think the simple approach is best. Let me finalize.

- Match 1: ('45', 's')

Actually wait: `\d+\.?\d*` — for '12.34.56', it would match '12.34' (greedy), then '.56' starts with '.' which isn't \d+, so it wouldn't match. Let me use a more robust approach:
1. Strip whitespace
2. Handle negative sign at the start
3. Use a regex to tokenize: `(\d+\.?\d*)([dhms])?`
4. Check that all matched text covers the entire string (after removing the sign)
5. If not, raise ValueError

Actually, let me think about this differently. A cleaner approach:

```python
import re

def parse_duration(s: str) -> int | float:
    if not s:
        return 0
    
    s = s.strip().lower()
    
    # Handle leading negative
    negative = False
    if s.startswith('-'):
        negative = True
        s = s[1:]
    
    if not s:
        raise ValueError("Empty duration")
    
    # Tokenize: find all number-unit pairs
    pattern = re.compile(r'(\d+\.?\d*)([dhms])?')
    matches = pattern.findall(s)
    
    if not matches:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid duration: {s}")
    
    # Verify the entire string was consumed
    reconstructed = ''.join(num + (unit or '') for num, unit in matches)
    if reconstructed != s:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid duration: {s}")
    
    # Also check: if there's no unit at all and only one token, it's seconds
    # Actually, the regex handles this: if no unit, unit is '' (empty string from optional group)
    
    multipliers = {'d': 86400, 'h': 3600, 'm': 60, 's': 1}
    
    total = 0
    for num, unit in matches:
        value = float(num)
        if unit == '':
            unit = 's'  # default to seconds
        elif unit not in multipliers:
            raise ValueError(f"Unknown unit: {unit}")
        total += value * multipliers[unit]
    
    if negative:
        total = -total
    
    # Return int if it's a whole number
    if total == int(total):
        return int(total)
    return total
```

Wait, there's an issue. `re.findall` with a pattern that has groups returns tuples of the groups. If the second group doesn't match, it returns empty string ''. Let me verify:

Final function:

```python
import re

def parse_duration(s: str) -> int | float:
    """Parse a duration string like '1h30m', '45s', or '2d4h' into seconds."""
    if s is None:
        raise ValueError("Duration cannot be None")
    
    s = str(s).strip().lower()
    if not s:
        return 0
    
    # Remove all internal whitespace
    s = re.sub(r'\s+', '', s)
    
    # Handle leading negative
    negative = False
    if s.startswith('-'):
        negative = True
        s = s[1:]
        if not s:
            raise ValueError("Invalid duration: '-'")
    
    # Tokenize
    pattern = re.compile(r'(\d+\.?\d*|\.\d+)([dhmsw])?')
    matches = pattern.findall(s)
    
    if not matches:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid duration: '{s}'")
    
    # Verify full string was consumed
    reconstructed = ''.join(num + (unit or '') for num, unit in matches)
    if reconstructed != s:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid duration: '{s}'")
    
    multipliers = {'w': 604800, 'd': 86400, 'h': 3600, 'm': 60, 's': 1}
    
    total = 0.0
    for num, unit in matches:
        value = float(num)
        multiplier = multipliers.get(unit if unit else 's')
        total += value * multiplier
    
    if negative:
        total = -total
    
    return int(total) if total == int(total) else total
```

- But wait, this means '1.2' is treated as 1.2 seconds and '.3h' is 0.3 hours. That's... not ideal but also not a crash. The string is technically valid in our grammar. I think this is acceptable for "reasonable edge cases."

Actually, I realize there might be an issue. Let me trace through '1.2.3h' more carefully with findall:
- Position 0: Try to match. `\d+\.?\d*` matches '1.2' (1, then ., then 2). Unit `[dhmsw]?` — next char is '.', not a unit, so unit is ''. Match: ('1.2', '').
- Position 3 (after '1.2'): Try to match. `\d+\.?\d*` — next char is '.', not a digit. Try `\.\d+` — matches '.3'. Unit `[dhmsw]?` — next char is 'h', matches. Match: ('.3', 'h').
- Position 6: End of string.
- findall returns: [('1.2', ''), ('.3', 'h')]
- Reconstructed: '1.2' + '' + '.3' + 'h' = '1.2.3h' ✓

Question: Write a small Python command-line tool that removes duplicate lines from a file while preserving the original order, with an --ignore-case flag. Give me the script.

Thinking:
The user wants a small Python CLI tool that:
1. Removes duplicate lines from a file
2. Preserves original order (first occurrence wins)
3. Has an --ignore-case flag (when set, "Foo" and "foo" are considered duplicates)

Let me think about the design:

- Use argparse for CLI args
- Input: file path (positional arg), output could be stdout or a -o flag
- --ignore-case flag
- Read file line by line, track seen lines in a set, skip duplicates
- For --ignore-case, normalize with .lower() before checking
- Preserve original order by iterating sequentially and only keeping first occurrence

Let me think about edge cases:
- Empty file
- File with trailing newline
- Binary files (maybe just let it crash naturally or handle gracefully)
- The "small" constraint means I should keep it concise but functional

Let me draft the script:

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Remove duplicate lines from a file, preserving order."""
import argparse
import sys

def main():
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Remove duplicate lines from a file.")
    parser.add_argument("file", help="Input file (use '-' for stdin)")
    parser.add_argument("--ignore-case", "-i", action="store_true",
                        help="Treat lines differing only in case as duplicates")
    parser.add_argument("--output", "-o", default=None,
                        help="Output file (default: stdout)")
    args = parser.parse_args()

    # Open input
    if args.file == "-":
        infile = sys.stdin
    else:
        infile = open(args.file, "r")

    # Open output
    if args.output:
        outfile = open(args.output, "w")
    else:
        outfile = sys.stdout

    seen = set()
    try:
        for line in infile:
            key = line.lower() if args.ignore_case else line
            if key not in seen:
                seen.add(key)
                outfile.write(line)
    finally:
        if infile is not sys.stdin:
            infile.close()
        if outfile is not sys.stdout:
            outfile.close()

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Wait, the user said "small" so I should keep it tight. Let me also think about whether to include `-o` flag. The user didn't ask for it explicitly but it's natural for a CLI tool. I'll include it since it makes the tool actually useful (otherwise you'd have to redirect stdout which is fine too). Actually, let me keep it minimal but include `-o` since it's one line and makes the tool more usable.

Let me also consider: should I use `line.rstrip('\n')` or keep the full line? Keeping the full line (with newline) is better because:
- It preserves exact formatting
- Writing it back with `outfile.write(line)` maintains the structure
- For the key comparison, I should strip the newline so that "foo\n" and "foo" (if last line has no newline) are still compared correctly... Actually no, if we're reading lines, each line will have its newline. The last line might not. For comparison purposes, I should strip the trailing newline from the key to handle edge cases where the last line lacks a newline but matches a previous line that has one.

Actually, let me reconsider. If the file is:
```
foo
bar
foo
```
Each line read is "foo\n", "bar\n", "foo\n". The key would be "foo\n" both times, so it works. But what if the last line is "foo" without newline? Then it wouldn't match "foo\n" from earlier. Let me strip for the key:

```python

```

Hmm, but then I'm writing the original `line` (with newline), which is correct for preserving output.

Actually, let me just keep it simple. The standard `for line in file` iteration gives lines with trailing newlines (except possibly the last). For a "small" tool, I'll strip for comparison but write the original. This handles the edge case cleanly.

Question: Describe exactly two approaches to detecting when a language model is overthinking a problem. One paragraph each, two approaches total.

Thinking:
The user wants me to describe exactly two approaches to detecting when a language model is overthinking a problem. One paragraph each, two approaches total.

Let me think about what "overthinking" means in the context of a language model. It means the model is generating unnecessarily complex, verbose, or circular reasoning for a problem that has a simple or direct answer. It might:
- Restate the question multiple times
- Consider trivially irrelevant edge cases
- Build elaborate frameworks for simple tasks
- Hedge excessively
- Go in circles
- Apply sophisticated analytical frameworks to questions that don't need them
- Generate responses much longer than necessary
- Introduce unnecessary caveats and qualifications
- Break simple problems into excessive sub-problems

Now, two approaches to DETECTING this:

Approach 1: Response complexity vs. problem complexity ratio analysis. You could measure the complexity of the problem (e.g., number of constraints, ambiguity level, required knowledge depth) against the complexity of the response (e.g., token count, number of distinct concepts introduced, branching factor of reasoning). If the response complexity significantly exceeds what the problem demands, that's a signal of overthinking. You could operationalize this by comparing the response length/structure to what a "minimal correct answer" would require, or by measuring whether the model introduces more sub-questions than the original question contains.

Approach 2: Self-consistency and convergence testing. Ask the model the same problem multiple times (or reframe it slightly) and measure whether the core answer stays stable while the framing/justification keeps changing and expanding. Overthinking manifests as the model generating different elaborate justifications for the same simple conclusion, or as the "thinking" portion growing while the "answer" portion stays the same. You could also test by asking the model to simplify its own response iteratively—if it cannot reduce the response to a core answer without losing correctness, the complexity might be justified; if it can strip away most of it and arrive at the same answer, it was overthinking.

Let me refine these into clear, distinct paragraphs. The user wants exactly two paragraphs, one per approach. Let me make sure they're distinct and well-articulated.

Actually, let me reconsider. The Brainstormer persona is active here, but the user's request is very specific and constrained: "exactly two approaches," "one paragraph each, "two approaches total." This is a precise formatting request. I should honor it exactly. The persona's "seek novelty" aspect can inform HOW I frame the approaches to make them interesting and non-obvious, but the constraint is firm: two paragraphs, two approaches.

Let me draft:

Paragraph 1: Complexity-to-necessity ratio. Measure the structural complexity of the output (number of distinct logical moves, introduced concepts, hedging markers, restatements of the prompt) against a baseline of what the problem's actual information-theoretic demands are. If a question has one clear answer path but the response branches into seven sub-considerations, three of which are irrelevant, the ratio flags overthinking. Operationally: parse the response into atomic reasoning steps, tag each as "necessary for correctness" or "ornamental/hedging/circular," and compute the ratio.

Paragraph 2: Iterative self-simplification test. Prompt the model to progressively strip its own answer—ask "Can you say this in half the words?" or "What is the one sentence that contains the answer?" If the model can compress its elaborate response into a short statement without losing any correct content, the removed material was overthinking. If compression causes the answer to become wrong or incomplete, the complexity was load-bearing. This is a functional test: overthinking is detected by the gap between the model's first-pass response and its irreducible core under compression pressure.

These are distinct: one is a static structural analysis of a single output, the other is a dynamic compression test across multiple outputs. Good.

Let me write the final response now—exactly two paragraphs, no preamble, no list formatting, just two paragraphs.

- Pattern: `\s*([+-]?\d*\.?\d+)\s*([hdms])\s*`

Let me use regex:
```python
import re

def parse_duration(s):
    if not s or not s.strip():
        raise ValueError("Empty duration string")
    
    s = s.strip()
    
    pattern = r'([+-]?\d+\.?\d*)\s*([hdmsHDMS])'
    matches = re.findall(pattern, s)
    
    # Check that the entire string is accounted for
    reconstructed = ''.join(f"{num}{unit}" for num, unit in matches)
    if re.sub(r'\s+', '', s) != reconstructed:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid duration string: '{s}'")
    
    unit_seconds = {'d': 86400, 'h': 3600, 'm': 60, 's': 1}
    
    total = 0
    for num, unit in matches:
        total += float(num) * unit_seconds[unit.lower()]
    
    # Return int if whole number, float otherwise
    return int(total) if total == int(total) else total
```

- Let me use `([+-]?\d+\.?\d*|[+-]?\.\d+)` 

Actually, simpler: `([+-]?\d*\.?\d+)` - this matches "45", "1.5", ".5", "-3", "1."... wait, "1." would match as "1." with the \d+ consuming "1" and \.?\d* consuming ".". Hmm, that's a problem.

Let me use: `([+-]?(?:\d+\.?\d*|\.\d+))` - this handles "45", "1.5", ".5", "1." (as 1.0 effectively). Actually "1." with `\d+\.?\d*` would match "1." - the \d+ matches "1", \.? matches ".", \d* matches empty. That's fine, float("1.") = 1.0 in Python.

Actually, a cleaner approach: use re.finditer and check that matches span the entire string (accounting for whitespace).

Let me write the final version:

```python
import re

def parse_duration(s):
    """
    Parse a duration string like '1h30m', '45s', '2d3h' into total seconds.
    
    Supported units: d (days), h (hours), m (minutes), s (seconds).
    Case-insensitive. Fractional values allowed (e.g., '1.5h').
    """
    if not isinstance(s, str):
        raise TypeError(f"Expected string, got {type(s).__name__}")
    
    s = s.strip()
    if not s:
        raise ValueError("Empty duration string")
    
    pattern = re.compile(r'([+-]?(?:\d+\.?\d*|\.\d+))\s*([dhmsDHMS])')
    matches = list(pattern.finditer(s))
    
    if not matches:
        raise ValueError(f"No valid duration components in '{s}'")
    
    # Verify the entire string is consumed (ignoring whitespace)
    expected = re.sub(r'\s+', '', s)
    actual = ''.join(m.group(0).replace(' ', '') for m in matches)
    if expected != actual:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid duration string: '{s}'")
    
    unit_seconds = {'d': 86400, 'h': 3600, 'm': 60, 's': 1}
    
    total = 0
    for m in matches:
        value = float(m.group(1))
        unit = m.group(2).lower()
        total += value * unit_seconds[unit]
    
    return int(total) if total == int(total) else total
```

Hmm, the "entire string consumed" check is a bit tricky with whitespace. Let me think...

If s = "1h 30m", after strip it's "1h 30m". The regex with `\s*` between number and unit would match "1h" and " 30m". The actual would be "1h30m" and expected (whitespace removed) would be "1h30m". That works.

If s = "1h30", the regex would only match "30"... wait no. Let me trace:
- pattern.finditer("1h30")
- First match: "1h" (number=1, unit=h)
- Then "30" remains - no unit, so no second match
- matches = ["1h"]
- expected = "1h30" (no whitespace to remove)
- actual = "1h"
- "1h30" != "1h" → raises ValueError ✓

If s = "abc":
- No matches → raises ValueError ✓

If s = "1h30m":
- matches: "1h", "30m"
- expected = "1h30m", actual = "1h30m" ✓
- total = 3600 + 1800 = 5400 ✓

If s = "  2H 45m  ":
- After strip: "2H 45m"
- matches: "2H", "45m"
- expected = "2H45m", actual = "2H45m" ✓
- total = 7200 + 2700 = 9900 ✓

Looks good. Let me also add a docstring with examples and maybe a few test cases in comments.

One more edge case: what about "1h30m15s" with the last component being just seconds? That's fine.

Question: Write a small Python command-line tool that removes duplicate lines from a file while preserving the original order, with an --ignore-case flag. Give me the script.

Thinking:
The user wants a small Python command-line tool that:
1. Removes duplicate lines from a file
2. Preserves original order
3. Has an `--ignore-case` flag
4. Should be a command-line tool (so argparse)

Let me think about the design:

- Use `argparse` for CLI
- Input: a file path (positional argument)
- Output: print to stdout by default, maybe also an `-o`/`--output` flag
- `--ignore-case` flag: when set, "Duplicate" and "duplicate" are considered the same line
- Preserve original order: iterate lines, track seen lines in a set, skip if already seen

Let me think about edge cases:
- Should we strip trailing newlines? Yes, we should compare without the trailing newline but preserve it in output.
- What about the `--ignore-case` flag? We normalize to lowercase for comparison but keep the original text for output.
- Should we support `stdin` if no file is given? That'd be nice but the user said "from a file." I'll keep it simple but maybe add stdin support as a bonus.

Let me draft the script:

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Remove duplicate lines from a file, preserving original order."""

import argparse
import sys


def deduplicate(lines, ignore_case=False):
    seen = set()
    result = []
    for line in lines:
        key = line.lower() if ignore_case else line
        if key not in seen:
            seen.add(key)
            result.append(line)
    return result


def main():
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Remove duplicate lines from a file while preserving order.")
    parser.add_argument("file", help="Input file (use '-' for stdin)")
    parser.add_argument("-o", "--output", help="Output file (default: stdout)")
    parser.add_argument("--ignore-case", action="store_true", help="Case-insensitive comparison")
    args = parser.parse_args()

    if args.file == "-":
        lines = sys.stdin.readlines()
    else:
        with open(args.file, "r") as f:
            lines = f.readlines()

    deduped = deduplicate(lines, ignore_case=args.ignore_case)

    if args.output:
        with open(args.output, "w") as f:
            f.writelines(deduped)
    else:
        sys.stdout.writelines(deduped)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

This is clean, small, and does exactly what's asked. Let me evaluate:

- Is it "small"? Yes, ~30 lines of actual logic.
- Does it preserve order? Yes, we iterate in order and only skip if already seen.
- Does `--ignore-case` work? Yes, we lowercase for the key but keep original for output.
- Is it a proper CLI tool? Yes, argparse with help text.

I think this is good. Let me also think about whether to strip trailing newlines before comparison. Actually, `readlines()` keeps the `\n`, and we compare full lines including the newline. Two lines that differ only in trailing whitespace (like one has `\r\n` vs `\n`) would be treated as different. Should I normalize that? For a "small" tool, I think it's fine to compare the raw lines. But let me add a small note or just handle `\r\n` vs `\n` by stripping and re-adding... Actually, let's keep it simple. The user said "small tool." I'll compare the lines as-is (including newline). That's the most predictable behavior.

Let me finalize my response.

Question: Describe exactly two approaches to detecting when a language model is overthinking a problem. One paragraph each, two approaches total.

Thinking:
The user wants me to describe exactly two approaches to detecting when a language model is overthinking a problem. One paragraph each, two approaches total. This is a constrained creative/analytical task.

Let me think about what "overthinking" means in the context of a language model:
- Producing unnecessarily long responses
- Introducing irrelevant considerations
- Hedging excessively
- Going in circles
- Adding caveats that don't change the answer
- Structuring responses with unnecessary taxonomy
- Addressing edge cases that aren't relevant
- Repeating the same point in different words
- Meta-commentary about the problem rather than solving it

Now, what are two distinct APPROACHES to DETECTING this?

Approach 1: Response compression/ablation testing. You could measure whether removing portions of the response changes its quality. If you can cut 70% of the response and the answer is still complete and correct, the model was overthinking. This is an information-theoretic or entropy-based measure. You could also compare the response to a "minimal sufficient answer" - what's the shortest formulation that fully addresses the query? The ratio between actual length and minimal sufficient length gives you an "overthinking score."

Approach 2: Self-consistency / convergence analysis. If a model is overthinking, its internal reasoning (if observable) or its successive outputs show a pattern of "diminishing returns" - the first few sentences/paragraphs carry 95% of the information value, and the rest is filler, restatement, or tangential exploration. You could detect this by tracking information novelty per token or per sentence. Once the marginal information gain drops below a threshold, the model has "finished thinking" and is now just generating more tokens than necessary. Another angle: if you prompt the model multiple times and the core answer is identical across all runs but the padding varies, that's a signal of overthinking.

Wait, let me make sure these are genuinely distinct approaches:
1. Comparative/compression-based: Compare output to a minimal sufficient answer. Measure the "waste ratio."
2. Information-flow/gradient-based: Track the marginal information gain per unit of output. Detect the point where information density drops below a threshold.

These are distinct. The first is a holistic comparison (end-state measurement). The second is a process-level measurement (tracking the curve of information contribution).

Let me write this as exactly two paragraphs, one per approach. I need to be precise and clear. Let me draft:

Paragraph 1 (Compression/ablation approach): One approach is minimal-sufficiency compression: generate the response, then systematically ablate it—removing sentences, paragraphs, or entire sections—and check whether the response still fully and correctly answers the original query. The "overthinking ratio" is the fraction of the response that can be removed without degrading completeness or correctness. A model that produces a 400-word answer to a question fully answerable in 40 words has an overthinking ratio of roughly 0.9, signaling that most of its output was redundant elaboration rather than necessary reasoning. This method is external and post-hoc: it doesn't require access to the model's internal states, only the final text and a reliable judgment (human or automated) of what constitutes a complete answer.

Paragraph 2 (Marginal information gain / information density tracking): A second approach is marginal-information-density tracking during generation. You monitor the response as it unfolds and measure, for each new sentence or clause, how much genuinely new information it contributes relative to what has already been said—whether by semantic similarity scoring against preceding text, by checking whether a key point is being restated in different words, or by testing whether removing that sentence would change the answer's logical content. Overthinking manifests as a sharp drop in marginal information gain: the model has reached its "settled answer" early and then continues generating hedging, restatements, tangential caveats, or meta-commentary that add negligible new content. Plotting information gain per token over the course of the response reveals a characteristic curve that plateaus or decays, and the point where it crosses a low threshold marks the boundary between actual reasoning and overthinking.

These are good. Let me refine for clarity and ensure they're exactly two paragraphs. Let me also make sure the language is precise and not itself "overthinking" the answer—ironic if I pad the response.

6. Fractional values: '1.5h' → 5400 seconds. This seems reasonable.

Let me think about the regex approach:
- Pattern: `\s*([+-]?\d*\.?\d+)\s*([hdms])\s*`
- Find all matches
- Check that the entire string is consumed
- Sum up the seconds

Actually, let me think more carefully. The pattern should be:
- Optional sign at the start
- Number (int or float)
- Unit letter (h, m, s, d)
- Repeat

Let me use regex:
```python
import re

def parse_duration(s):
    if not s or not s.strip():
        raise ValueError("Empty duration string")
    
    s = s.strip()
    
    pattern = r'([+-]?\d+\.?\d*)\s*([hdmsHDMS])'
    matches = re.findall(pattern, s)
    
    # Check that the entire string is accounted for
    reconstructed = ''.join(f"{num}{unit}" for num, unit in matches)
    if re.sub(r'\s+', '', s) != reconstructed:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid duration string: '{s}'")
    
    unit_seconds = {'d': 86400, 'h': 3600, 'm': 60, 's': 1}
    
    total = 0
    for num, unit in matches:
        total += float(num) * unit_seconds[unit.lower()]
    
    # Return int if whole number, float otherwise
    return int(total) if total == int(total) else total
```

What about just '30' with no unit? I'll say that's invalid - you need a unit.

Thinking:
Thinking Process:

1.  **Identify the core question:** The user is asking for the SI unit of magnetic flux.
2.  **Recall knowledge about magnetic flux:**
    *   Magnetic flux ($\Phi_B$) is defined as the surface integral of the magnetic field ($B$) over an area ($A$). $\Phi_B = \int B \cdot dA$.
    *   The SI unit of magnetic field ($B$) is the Tesla (T).
    *   The SI unit of area ($A$) is the square meter ($m^2$).
    *   Therefore, the unit of magnetic flux is Tesla-square meter ($T \cdot m^2$).
    *   There is a specific named SI unit for this quantity.
3.  **Identify the named unit:** The unit is the Weber.
4.  **Verify the symbol:** The symbol is Wb.
5.  **Formulate the answer:** The SI unit of magnetic flux is the Weber.
6.  **Format the output:** The user requested a specific format: "Exact Answer: <your answer>".

*Self-Correction/Double Check:*
*   Is it Gauss? No, Gauss is CGS.
*   Is it Tesla? No, Tesla is magnetic flux density (magnetic field strength).
*   Is it Weber? Yes. $1 \text{ Wb} = 1 \text{ T} \cdot \text{m}^2$.

7.  **Final Output Generation:**
    Exact Answer: Weber (or Wb)

    The prompt asks for "Exactly one line" at the end. I will provide the name.

    *Drafting the response:*
    The SI unit of magnetic flux is the weber (symbol: Wb). It is defined as the flux that, linking a circuit of one turn, would produce in it an electromotive force of 1 volt if it were reduced to zero at a uniform rate in 1 second.

    *Final Line:*
    Exact Answer: Weber

Thinking:
Thinking Process:

1.  **Identify the core entity and question:** The user is asking for the year the Treaty of Tordesillas was signed.
2.  **Retrieve knowledge about the Treaty of Tordesillas:**
    *   What is it? A treaty between Spain and Portugal.
    *   Purpose: To divide the newly discovered lands outside Europe between the two powers.
    *   Date: June 7, 1494.
3.  **Verify the date:**
    *   Source check (internal knowledge): The Treaty of Tordesillas was signed on June 7, 1494.
4.  **Format the output:**
    *   The user requested a specific format: "Exact Answer: <your answer>" on the last line.
    *   The answer is 1494.
5.  **Final Output Construction:**
    *   Brief thought (optional but requested "Think briefly if you need to").
    *   Final line: Exact Answer: 1494.

Double check: Is there any ambiguity? No. Is the year 1494? Yes.

Drafting response:
The Treaty of Tordesillas was signed on June 7, 1494.

Exact Answer: 1494

Thinking:
Thinking Process:

1.  **Identify the core entity and question:** The user is asking for the surname of the author of the novel 'Stoner', published in 1965.

    *   Query: "Stoner novel 1965 author"
    *   Result: The novel *Stoner* was written by John Williams.
    *   Publication date: 1965.
3.  **Extract the surname:**
    *   Author's full name: John Williams.
    *   Surname: Williams.
4.  **Format the output:**
    *   Constraint: End with exactly one line: "Exact Answer: <your answer>".
    *   Answer: Williams.
5.  **Final Verification:**
    *   Novel: *Stoner*.
    *   Year: 1965.
    *   Author: John Williams.
    *   Surname: Williams.
    *   Format check: "Exact Answer: Williams".

6.  **Construct response:**
    *   Brief thought (internal): The author is John Williams.
    *   Final line: Exact Answer: Williams.

    *Self-Correction/Double Check:* Is there any other famous novel called 'Stoner' from 1965? No, John Williams' *Stoner* is the definitive one, though it was a commercial failure upon release and later became a cult classic.

    *Result:* Williams.

    *Drafting the response:*
    The author of the 1965 novel *Stoner* is John Williams.

    Exact Answer: Williams

Thinking:
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Identify the core question**: The user asks "How many bones are in the adult human foot?"
2.  **Recall/verify anatomical knowledge**: 
   - The human foot is divided into three main regions: tarsals, metatarsals, and phalanges.
   - Tarsals: 7 bones (talus, calcaneus, navicular, cuboid, medial cuneiform, intermediate cuneiform, lateral cuneiform)
   - Metatarsals: 5 bones
   - Phalanges: 14 bones (2 for the big toe, 3 for each of the other 4 toes: 2 + 3*4 = 14)
   - Total: 7 + 5 + 14 = 26 bones per foot.
